The OBR might judge that there is not yet enough evidence to justify a significant downgrade to
its productivity and growth forecasts this autumn. However, making fiscal policy based on an
economic forecast that continues to be an outlier might come with its own risks: it is important
for the OBR’s forecasts to be seen as credible. Consistent over-optimism might risk that
reputation with voters and markets, and eventually affect the cost of government borrowing.
15 In order to capture the impact of a possible productivity downgrade, this estimate only accounts for changes
stemming from the difference in real growth forecasts between Barclays and the OBR March forecast. The
Barclays forecast also includes differences in other variables relative to the OBR, which are included in
calculations in Section 3.4 but are not included here.
© The Institute for Fiscal Studies, October 2025

Risks and challenges for the public finances 109
Feeble productivity growth is a risk that could continue to push debt up as a share of national
income – and that is a risk that a prudent and forward-looking Chancellor should take into
account, whether or not the OBR chooses to revise its productivity assumption on this particular
occasion.
3.6 Policy risks
The outlook for the public finances is always inherently uncertain, and will not be shaped by the
economic trajectory alone. In addition to the shifting economic environment, there are a range of
uncertainties around policy that may affect the strain on the public finances over the next several
years. Some areas of public spending could need significant top-ups if circumstances do not
develop in the government’s favour, while social security spending, in the absence of reform,
could rise more – or less – quickly than is forecast, particularly given that the drivers of the
increases in claims of health-related benefits among children, working-age households and
pensioners are far from understood. On the revenue side, there are reasons to think that tax
revenue could either exceed or fall short of levels currently forecast. These factors are unlikely
to influence tax or spending decisions at the Budget (unless they are reflected in the OBR central
forecast), but could heavily influence the actual course of spending, revenues and borrowing
over the next five years.
In this section, we discuss a number of policy risks, or reasons why spending or revenues might
deviate from forecasts, focusing on their consequences for borrowing in 2029–30, the target year
for the fiscal rules.
Spending risks
Public sector productivity
The government’s spending plans rest on ambitious planned improvements in the productivity of
public services: spending plans assume that more medical procedures can be performed, more
court cases dealt with and more taxes collected for the same level of funding for the government
departments responsible. Historically, the opposite has more often happened: over time, it has
become more expensive to provide the same level of public services, not less. It may be that this
parliament will be an exception. For one thing, sizeable funding increases for public services
since the COVID-19 pandemic have so far not resulted in a corresponding increase in outputs,
and there should be scope for catch-up growth. Almost two-thirds of the planned public service
productivity gains are set to come from the NHS in England, where plans imply that hospitals
will return to 2019 levels of productivity only by 2028–29. This may – perhaps even should – be
achievable. High levels of investment spending compared with historical averages may help with
this. New technologies, and in particular artificial intelligence, may also boost productivity
across a range of tasks in the public sector.
© The Institute for Fiscal Studies, October 2025

Risks and challenges for the public finances 110
However, there is a counterpoint to this optimistic case, and a risk that the historical pattern
instead repeats itself and productivity falls, or does not rise as quickly as spending plans assume.
In that case, the government would have two choices: it could maintain its spending plans and
allow public services to deteriorate, potentially falling short of ambitious commitments made in
the 2024 manifesto; or it could top up spending plans to compensate.
As set out in Chapter 6, if the outputs that the public sector is able to produce with a given level
of funding evolve according to their long-run historical trend (a slight fall over time) and the
government still wanted to achieve the level of output assumed at the 2025 Spending Review, it
could require tens of billions of additional spending in 2029–30.
Of course, it is conceivable that the government will be able to find more efficiencies in the
public sector than anticipated, in which case the planned level of output could be achieved with
less funding. In that case, the government could allow service providers to use the funding freed
up in this way to do more or do it better – or claw back some funding to maintain the same level
of output planned in the Spending Review while spending less.
Ambitious targets for productivity improvements are not the only source of pressure on
departmental spending plans, though. In the central scenario presented in Section 3.4, economy-
wide inflation – as measured by the GDP deflator, the measure of prices used to calculate the
real-terms generosity of spending plans for public services – is well above the March 2025
forecast. If inflation does indeed turn out higher than expected, this would increase pressure on
the government to top up spending plans to maintain their real-terms generosity, rather than
expecting public services to absorb higher costs – and, very likely, demands for higher pay
awards – within their existing cash budgets.
Defence spending
The July Spending Review allocated budgets to government departments up to 2028–29. In that
year, the government plans to spend 2.6% of national income on defence – 0.3% of national
income more than it did in 2024–25. Subsequently, at the NATO Summit in The Hague at the
end of June, the government, alongside many of its NATO allies, committed to increase
spending to 3.5% of national income by 2035. No commitment was made on the path to get to
this figure.
Chapter 7 sets out alternative paths for defence spending, ranging from a scenario where the
Spending Review allocation to defence is topped up to move steadily towards the 3.5% target, to
one where all increases beyond 2.6% are deferred to the next parliament. Either of these could be
consistent with the government meeting its 3.5% commitment in 2035, but this choice over the
profile of spending could make a sizeable difference to defence spending in 2029–30 – the gap
between those two paths is £7.9 billion in that year.
© The Institute for Fiscal Studies, October 2025

Risks and challenges for the public finances 111
The central scenario in Section 3.4, like the OBR’s March forecast, takes the government’s
‘pencilled-in’ spending plans for public services after the end of the Spending Review period as
given. Spending totals after 2028–29 have not yet been allocated to individual departments. A
higher defence budget could, in theory, come at the expense of lower spending by another
department – although with modest overall planned increases to day-to-day public service
spending in 2029–30, at 1% in real terms, this would likely imply difficult trade-offs. If plans
were instead topped up, borrowing might need to be higher.
Notably, the defence budget – and, in particular, top-ups to the defence budget – include a large
share of capital spending relative to day-to-day spending: the £7.9 billion top-up to get from the
heavily backloaded path to the steadily increasing path would almost certainly include at least
40% capital spending, and the share could be much higher. This means that the impact of any
top-up on the government’s headroom against the borrowing rule is likely to be smaller than the
impact on the debt target – although at least in the Barclays central scenario we present in
Section 3.4, it is actually the latter that is more constraining.
Health- and disability-related benefits
Spending to support children and working-age adults with disabilities and long-term health
conditions has risen much more sharply since the COVID-19 pandemic than it did over the
2010s. This largely reflects fast-rising caseloads. We have already discussed the government’s
choice not to go ahead with planned cuts which were incorporated into the OBR’s March 2025
forecast, and the impact of these policy reversals on overall spending. We also discussed the
impact of higher consumer price inflation on social security spending – this also applies to
benefits to support people with disabilities and long-term health conditions.
However, for a given policy environment and inflation forecast, the future path of spending will
crucially depend on caseloads and awards. Sharply rising caseloads for child disability support
have implications for future spending on support for adults with disabilities and health
conditions: Chapter 5 notes that more than four in five teenagers who applied for personal
independence payment when they were about to age out of the child disability system were
successful, and the vast majority received an award that was the same as or higher than their
child disability living allowance. The OBR’s March 2025 forecast assumes that health- and
disability-related welfare spending continues to grow at a faster rate than it did pre-pandemic,
but not as fast as it did over the last five years.
Of course, there is uncertainty around this forecast, in both directions. If spending on child
disability benefits and adult incapacity and disability benefits returned to the growth rate seen on
average over the second half of the 2010s, spending in today’s prices could be £3.7 billion lower
than in our scenario in 2029–30, the year the fiscal rules bind. Notably, this would still be
slightly above the level in the OBR’s March 2025 forecast, which included the government’s
© The Institute for Fiscal Studies, October 2025

Risks and challenges for the public finances 112
now-abandoned reforms. Conversely, if the high growth rate seen over the five years to 2024–25
continues unabated, spending in 2029–30 would exceed our scenario by £17.5 billion in today’s
prices.
In principle, a lower growth rate in spending could come about either through a deliberate policy
effort, or through changes to the underlying drivers of spending, notably population health and
would-be claimants’ choice to apply. It is worth noting, though, that past policy efforts to reduce
spending on health- and disability-related benefits have often run into difficulties: this is not just
because of political opposition as in the case of this Spring’s proposals. The introduction of
personal independence payment (PIP) in 2013 had the aim of delivering a 20% reduction in
caseloads and spending (and was scored by the OBR at the time as doing so). Instead, the OBR
now judges that the reform ended up increasing spending (Office for Budget Responsibility,
2019).
Other social security spending
There is also well-publicised pressure on the Chancellor to increase the generosity of the
working-age benefit system, specifically calls to abolish – or at least reform – the two-child limit
in universal credit and the associated household benefit cap. Removing both would be a well-
targeted way to reduce child poverty: estimates suggest that over the longer term, this would
reduce child poverty by around 550,000 children each year (Henry and Wernham, 2024). But an
obvious downside is that it would add around £3 billion a year to spending.
As discussed in Chapter 8, there is a proposal on the table that could deliver savings to the
working-age benefits bill. This is the government’s proposal to replace new style jobseeker’s
allowance (NS JSA) and new style employment & support allowance (NS ESA) with a single
new ‘unemployment insurance’ (UI) benefit. While the proposal would set UI at a much higher
weekly rate for those who are actively seeking work, the time-limiting of the benefit – which
will reduce the incomes of many of those who would otherwise have received NS ESA – could
deliver savings overall. As set out in Chapter 8, after any transitional protections had expired,
time-limiting the benefit to 12 months would save around £0.6 billion a year. Restricting it to 6
months would save around £1.4 billion a year.
Revenue risks
Closing the tax gap?
Discussions of revenue risks usually focus on the underlying economic drivers of taxable
activity. But not all taxable activity actually is taxed successfully: this difference between tax
owed (as estimated by HMRC) and paid is known as the tax gap.
© The Institute for Fiscal Studies, October 2025

Risks and challenges for the public finances 113
Measures to reduce this gap and ensure more of the tax that ought to be paid is actually paid are
a popular addition to the scorecard for any Chancellor; indeed, the official tax gap fell from
7.4% in 2005–06 to 5.3% in 2023–24. The OBR’s forecast assumes that measures announced in
the last two years shrink this by a further 0.4 percentage points by 2029–30, the target year of
the fiscal rules, raising an additional £6 billion.
There is substantial uncertainty around this. The fall in the tax gap has not been universal across
taxes and taxpayers: the tax gap for smaller companies’ corporation tax liabilities grew quickly
and was estimated at 40% in 2023–24. As set out in Chapter 4, this means that even returning
the gap to its level in 2017–18 would raise more than £10 billion in additional revenue in 2029–
30.An optimist may conclude that well-designed measures should be able to collect most or all
of this additional revenue. However, the causes of the sharp decline in compliance among
smaller companies are not well understood, meaning that designing policies to achieve this goal
may be much easier said than done. If these trends continued instead, or even spread to other
taxes or groups of taxpayers, the risk for revenues may be that the tax gap actually closes by less
than the forecast currently assumes.
Fuel duties
The OBR forecast assumes that rates of fuel duties will increase in line with RPI inflation, and
that the ‘temporary’ 5p per litre cut to duties will be allowed to expire in April 2026. This is
despite the facts that cash freezes, rather than inflation uprating, have become the normal
practice of Chancellors since 2011 and that the ‘temporary’ 5p cut was originally supposed to be
in place for only one year and yet has been in place since 2022. As set out in Chapter 4, fuel duty
revenue in 2025–26 (forecast to be £24.4 billion) is expected to be £17.4 billion lower than it
would have been had the rates been increased in line with RPI inflation since 2011.
This practice suggests that it is more likely that rates of fuel duties remain at their current cash
values for the next five years. Freezing duties at their current rates through to 2029–30 would
reduce revenues in that year by an estimated £5.5 billion. Indeed, such a freeze would lead to a
nominal decline in fuel duty receipts over time, as petrol and diesel vehicles become more fuel-
efficient and the shift to electric vehicles continues. If cash freezes are the Chancellor’s intent,
then she should announce them now so that taxpayers can plan on that basis. However, they
would also necessitate additional fiscal tightening elsewhere to comply with the fiscal rules to
make up for reduced fuel duty revenues.
Perhaps more likely is that the Chancellor will announce at either the Autumn Budget or the
Spring Forecast that rates will (for now) be frozen for one more year. This would reduce forecast
revenues in 2029–30 by £2.7 billion. The remaining £2.8 billion (£5.5 billion less £2.7 billion) in
2029–30 would then feed into later forecast vintages as subsequent ‘one more year’ freezes were
announced (assuming that is how policy evolves). In doing so, the costs of annual freezes would
© The Institute for Fiscal Studies, October 2025

Risks and challenges for the public finances 114
not appear in the forecast, removing the need for the same level of fiscal tightening. This would
not be a good way to conduct policy, although unfortunately it feels closer to being a fiscal
certainty than a fiscal risk.
3.7 Budget judgement
Diagnosis: the backdrop
The Chancellor faces many challenges. Economic growth has been weak since 2008, and
forecasts suggest that the economy will continue to expand only modestly over the next few
years. By UK standards, taxes are already at a historic high and rising further, while debt interest
spending is significantly elevated relative to the period from 2010 to 2021. Spending on
disability- and health-related benefits had been rising steadily, before sharply accelerating from
Summer 2021; this rise is expected to continue. The Prime Minister has made a commitment to
NATO allies to increase defence spending substantially as a share of national income over the
next decade. Many domestic public services are visibly struggling, perhaps most obviously the
NHS, the largest-spending public service, where the government has ambitious targets to reduce
waiting lists from their recent highs.
The Autumn 2024 Budget
In her first Budget, Rachel Reeves introduced a large package of tax rises. But these did not
coincide with a strengthening of the current budget position, as planned day-to-day spending on
public services was increased by an even larger amount. The decision to top up spending plans
was unsurprising, given the ambitions to improve public services laid out in Labour’s manifesto
and the tight – bordering on implausible – spending plans that Ms Reeves inherited from her
predecessor as Chancellor, Jeremy Hunt. What was more striking was how front-loaded the
additional spending was, with spending growth concentrated in 2024–25 and 2025–26 and much
slower planned growth from April 2026 onwards.
The Budget also included a major increase in planned public sector investment spending. This
boost was sufficient to maintain investment at around its current share of national income –
relatively high by the standards of recent decades – rather than allowing it to fall as Mr Hunt’s
plans implied. This decision has much to commend it: if the investment is delivered effectively,
it should improve the quality and resilience of public services in future years. The case for more
investment, however, is separate from the case for funding this additional investment through
borrowing, which Ms Reeves chose to do.
The resulting medium-term fiscal loosening in the Autumn 2024 Budget was large. It was on a
par with Rishi Sunak’s post-election Budget in March 2020, and in recent history surpassed only
by George Osborne’s pre-election Budget of March 2015.
© The Institute for Fiscal Studies, October 2025

Risks and challenges for the public finances 115
The Spring 2025 Forecast
Ms Reeves’s choice – of higher taxes, higher spending, higher borrowing, and hence higher debt
–was left broadly unchanged at the Spring Statement. The measures announced in March were
kept to around the minimum required to leave the outlook for borrowing broadly unchanged
from the previous Budget. That is to say, the Chancellor continued to meet her two main fiscal
rules, but only just. The current budget was forecast to reach a surplus of just £9.9 billion by
2029–30 – a slim margin, almost identical to that she had after her first Budget, leaving her
vulnerable to even modest future revisions in the fiscal outlook. While public sector net debt was
forecast to continue rising as a share of national income over the next five years, the
Chancellor’s preferred measure of debt – public sector net financial liabilities – was forecast to
fall slightly by 2029–30.
Headroom against the fiscal rules is tiny, and the debt rule at least is relatively loose. But the
ultimate economic constraint is not the fiscal rules themselves. In broad terms, debt is merely
forecast to be stable as a share of national income in five years’ time – not falling. History tells
us that adverse shocks tend to push debt up which means that, if debt is not to rise indefinitely,
Chancellors must aim to reduce debt in normal times. Moreover, while the cost of debt was low
during the 2010s, that is no longer the case. The March forecast projected debt interest spending
in 2025–26 of £111 billion. Among the broad functions of government spending, this figure is
only surpassed by what the government spends each year on the NHS and on the state pension.
And the £111 billion figure is £64 billion higher than the £47 billion forecast for debt interest
spending in 2025–26 made in the March 2022 Budget just three years ago. Amid a host of
factors behind the government’s ongoing fiscal challenges, this – that we are spending more than
double what we expected to spend on debt interest, some 2% of national income more – stands
out as one of the central issues.
Changes since March
Since March, the fiscal outlook has worsened. Some of this reflects policy reversals by the
government. Many more pensioners will once again be able to benefit from the winter fuel
payment from this winter. Planned disability benefit reforms have been watered down, and as a
result will deliver almost none of the savings that had been previously expected in 2029–30.
Overall, these two changes will add around £6 billion to spending in that year. Market
expectations of long-term interest rates, which remain volatile, have risen since March; given
elevated debt, this alone could increase (the forecast for) debt interest spending in 2029–30 by
around £5 billion. Most critically, having often been more optimistic than most about the UK’s
growth prospects – and, unfortunately, often too optimistic with hindsight – the OBR is widely
expected to downgrade its productivity growth forecasts. Each 0.1 percentage point reduction
per year would add roughly £7 billion to borrowing in 2029–30.
© The Institute for Fiscal Studies, October 2025

Risks and challenges for the public finances 116
If the OBR were to adopt something like the Barclays central forecast for the economy, then
alongside all other changes since March, borrowing might be on course to be around £95 billion
in 2029–30 – £22 billion higher than at the last OBR forecast. Such a downgrade would not be
particularly large in the grand scheme of things: official forecasts have seen a downward
revision to the outlook for the public finances that is larger than this on eight occasions since the
OBR first reported in June 2010. It would leave growth over the next three years similar to that
forecast by the Bank of England, and still above the average of independent forecasts surveyed
by the Treasury. A downgrade of this magnitude would, however, without offsetting fiscal
action, leave the current budget in deficit over the next five years, with a forecast shortfall of £12
billion in 2029–30, down from a surplus of £10 billion last March. The Chancellor’s borrowing
rule would therefore be breached and, because public sector net financial liabilities would be
forecast to rise as a share of national income in 2029–30, her debt rule would be breached too.
Prescription: so what is a Chancellor to do?
Faced with this situation, the Chancellor has three uncomfortable options: borrow more, cut
planned spending, or raise taxes further. The case for borrowing significantly more on a
permanent basis is weak. Doing so would leave Ms Reeves’s ‘non-negotiable’ and ‘iron-clad’
fiscal rules in disarray. There is nothing sacrosanct about these or indeed any set of fiscal rules.
But borrowing significantly more – and further loosening or abandoning the fiscal targets –
would leave debt on an ever-increasing path, which comes with real economic risks. While these
risks might not crystallise for a long time, with gilt markets in a febrile state the government’s
borrowing costs – already a major source of fiscal pressure, and high relative to other major
economies – could easily be pushed up further in the near term.
If a widely expected forecast downgrade does materialise next month, the Chancellor will
therefore need to find savings.
Spend less?
Reducing planned spending is an option – though a difficult one.
The Chancellor could look again at planned spending on social security benefits. However – as
set out above – her previous attempts to cut spending on pensioner benefits and working-age
benefits are now expected to deliver far smaller savings over this parliament than she initially
envisaged. Some other widely discussed proposals for reform – such as abolishing the two-child
limit and the household benefit cap – would, if implemented, increase social security spending,
whereas others, such as the government’s proposal for a new time-limited unemployment
insurance benefit, would likely deliver a net saving. The Chancellor could choose to abolish the
two-child limit and the household benefit cap and offset some of the increase in forecast
working-age benefit spending by also introducing the new unemployment insurance with a time
© The Institute for Fiscal Studies, October 2025

Risks and challenges for the public finances 117
limit of 6 months. This would redistribute away from those with long-term health conditions
who would have been eligible for the previous benefit for longer than 6 months and are not
eligible for universal credit (see Chapter 8), and towards families with more than two children
who do receive universal credit. However, this would be a re-prioritisation of existing spending,
rather than a means to reduce overall social security spending in pursuit of fiscal consolidation.
Ms Reeves could also reopen the plans for spending on public services over the next few years,
with a view to trimming certain departmental budgets. But these plans were only agreed in June
and were set as multi-year settlements in order to aid departments’ ability to plan ahead.
Revising them so soon seems unlikely and would sit oddly with the Chancellor’s emphasis on
stability. In addition, there are reasons to think calls for top-ups to the Spending Review
settlements are likely (although perhaps not until the next Spending Review, which is scheduled
for 2027) – from the risk of productivity improvements falling short of ambitious targets (see
Chapter 6), to pressures to go further and faster on defence spending increases (see Chapter 7),
to calls to maintain the real-terms generosity of existing plans in the face of higher inflation.
The year in which the fiscal rules bind, 2029–30, does not fall within the current Spending
Review period, and thus the Chancellor could theoretically pencil in spending cuts for that year
without specifying how they would fall on individual departments. However, this kind of move
would lack credibility. With no fundamental reason why real growth in day-to-day spending
should fall sharply between the third and fourth years in the forecast, there would be little reason
to think any spending cuts announced for only 2029–30 would be stuck to and not undone at the
next Spending Review. The previous government scheduled in similarly implausible spending
plans for the current parliament, which were described as ‘worse than fiction’ by the Office for
Budget Responsibility’s chair, Richard Hughes, and topped up very substantially at the Autumn
2024 Budget. The Chancellor would be unwise to mimic this fiscal illusion.
Raise taxes?
Further tax rises are a likely option – despite manifesto commitments.
Given the inadvisability of permanently higher borrowing and the challenges in cutting spending
substantially, further tax rises are likely even if the downgrade is relatively modest in size.
While taxes are already high by UK historical standards – and already forecast to rise more –
most Western European and Scandinavian countries have successful economies with much
higher tax revenues as a share of national income. But the more taxes are raised, the more they
will distort behaviour, reducing growth and therefore standards of living. But as set out in
Chapter 4, there are opportunities here. The UK tax system is badly designed, so that the current
range of taxes do more damage to the economy than they need to. The risk is that unreformed
taxes are increased and, as a result, are even more harmful. The opportunity is that by first
reforming taxes
© The Institute for Fiscal Studies, October 2025

Risks and challenges for the public finances 118
and then, if deemed necessary, increasing them, greater revenue could be raised with
commensurately less damage to living standards. The Chancellor should confront the challenge
of tax reform, ensuring that any future increases are preceded by efforts to improve the design
and efficiency of the tax system.
Room for thinking ahead?
If Ms Reeves opts for spending cuts or tax rises, this raises the question of how large a fiscal
tightening she should opt for, and whether this should be accompanied by changes to the fiscal
framework. We consider each in turn.
The Chancellor could choose to implement just enough of a fiscal tightening to allow her to
continue meeting her fiscal rules without any headroom. On our central forecast, based on the
Barclays view of the likely performance of the economy over the next few years, this would
require a fiscal tightening of at least £17 billion in 2029–30. The rule that most constrains policy
in this scenario – where growth is expected to be weak towards the end of the forecast period in
particular – is the debt rule, rather than the borrowing rule. In other words, the Chancellor would
be required to aim for a current budget surplus – albeit a tiny one of £6 billion – in order to keep
her preferred measure of debt from rising by 2029–30, without cutting investment spending
plans. Another option would be to implement a fiscal tightening of £22 billion – i.e. sufficient to
offset the underlying increase in borrowing, and thereby restore headroom against the borrowing
rule to the slim £10 billion of headroom that was left in place in March.
Persisting with a low level of headroom or no headroom at all has the obvious advantage of
facilitating lower taxes or higher spending than what greater headroom would require. But it
leaves the Chancellor exposed to the risk that any future downward revisions to the outlook for
the public finances – revisions which are not uncommon – necessitate policy adjustments if the
fiscal targets are not to be breached. Overly frequent adjustments to tax and spending are not
conducive to good policymaking and can have adverse consequences for households and firms
affected by those changes. Moreover, widespread speculation about which taxes might rise – and
the resulting efforts by taxpayers to change their plans in anticipation – can also be costly.
The Chancellor’s ambition to create a more stable policy environment by holding only one fiscal
event each year is therefore welcome. It is an ambition that was shared, but not turned into a
reality, by both George Osborne and Philip Hammond.16 The more general commitment to
policy stability is also welcome. But it is important to note that it is not the existence of fiscal
16 ‘[The government] intends to move away from the significant fiscal policy changes announced at PBRs [Pre-
Budget Reports] in recent years’ (https://researchbriefings.files.parliament.uk/documents/SN05776/SN05776.pdf)
and ‘I am abolishing the autumn statement. No other major economy makes hundreds of tax changes twice a year,
and neither should we’ (https://hansard.parliament.uk/commons/2016-11-23/debates/4F39F2C9-583D-407B-
A529-3956F6A927F1/AutumnStatement).
© The Institute for Fiscal Studies, October 2025

Risks and challenges for the public finances 119
rules, or the role of the OBR in evaluating them, that has undermined it. Instead, volatility in
policy has stemmed from her decision to commit to meeting high-profile pass-or-fail fiscal
targets that are assessed twice a year, and her decision to set policy to meet them with narrow
margins. The former has been true of many of her predecessors in recent times, but having such
little headroom has been a phenomenon in UK fiscal policy only since Jeremy Hunt’s November
2022 Budget.
From this starting point, if the aim is to foster greater policy stability, there are several options
available to the Chancellor, each of which would make it easier to make changes to tax and
spending at only one fiscal event a year, without breaching her fiscal rules.
The first route, and by far the most straightforward one, would be to implement a fiscal
tightening that exceeds the deterioration in the underlying outlook in the autumn. This would
subsequently allow greater headroom to act in its intended role as a buffer. For example, a
tightening £20 billion bigger than the underlying increase in borrowing in 2029–30 would
increase the forecast current budget surplus from £10 billion to £30 billion, or 1% of national
income, a level of headroom that Chancellors commonly left themselves prior to 2022. This
would significantly reduce the chances of further policy action being required in the March 2026
Spring Forecast to continue to comply with the Chancellor’s borrowing rule. Of course, this
would not be costless: it would take higher taxes or lower spending to build this additional
headroom. Communicating the reason for those tax rises or spending cuts could be a challenge.
Other options are also available to the Chancellor. She could ask the OBR to produce only one
economic and fiscal forecast a year. This would automatically remove the possibility of missing
her fiscal rules in the spring, as now there would be no forecast against which to evaluate them.
Such a move would also have the advantage of being (relatively) easy to explain. However, this
would be a very blunt instrument: removing the Spring Forecast would reduce transparency and
make the UK an outlier among other advanced economies and international bodies. The UK has
had a legal requirement to publish two forecasts a year for half a century – since the passing of
then Secretary of State for Industry Tony Benn’s Industry Act 1975. It is only choices
government has made in recent years that have exacerbated such problems so much. In the
absence of a new OBR forecast, speculation about the size of a potentially required fiscal
tightening may well become more frenzied, less anchored in sound methodologies – especially
as the most recent official forecast could become considerably out of date – and go on for longer.
Even with only one forecast per year, with minimal headroom the government could find itself
in an extremely similar situation next year – the ‘fiscal Groundhog Day’ scenario.
Another option, if retaining the second forecast, would be to bring forward planned changes to
the operation of the fiscal targets, where the stated intention (if not our reading of the letter of
the actual legislation) is for the borrowing rule to be assessed against a range at the Spring Fiscal
© The Institute for Fiscal Studies, October 2025

Risks and challenges for the public finances 120
Forecast. Fiscal action would only be required at that point if the current budget were forecast to
be in deficit by more than 0.5% of national income. More modest forecast deficits could be left
until the autumn, when measures could be implemented if still needed to return to a current
budget surplus (see Emmerson, Miller and Zaranko (2025) for a discussion). However, these
measures would need to be brought forward to 2025–26, and to be applied to the debt rule as
well as the borrowing rule (given that the former may soon become the binding constraint on
policy), if they are to reduce excessive policy volatility. Ms Reeves could potentially combine
this with pre-announcing contingent measures – for example, stating that any shortfall that
persists in the autumn will be compensated by a rate rise in one particular tax – to reduce
uncertainty, and potentially ill-advised financial decision-making by those trying to anticipate a
future tax change. However, these benefits would only accrue if these contingent measures can
be communicated carefully and credibly to markets and taxpayers – not an easy task – and they
would add to the complexity of the fiscal framework.
In sum, the Chancellor’s stated aim of providing greater policy stability is a sensible one.
Scrapping the Spring Forecast would be one (rather blunt) means of avoiding over-correction in
the spring. Doing so would reduce transparency and would not avoid the risk of having to go
through the same charade next autumn – but would at least have the advantage of being simple
and easy to communicate. Bringing forward adjustments to the fiscal framework (perhaps
alongside rewritten legislation to clarify its operation) would be neater, but admittedly more
complex. Implementing a larger fiscal consolidation than strictly necessary, in order to build in a
larger amount of headroom, would likely be well received by bond markets (see Chapter 2),
would help to secure policy stability beyond just the spring and would have much to commend it
– but would have the unavoidable downside of requiring larger tax rises or spending cuts than
otherwise. It is for the Chancellor to judge how to weigh these competing considerations.

Options for tax increases 122
4. Options for tax increases
Stuart Adam, Isaac Delestre and Helen Miller (IFS)
The Chancellor, Rachel Reeves, is likely to raise taxes in the upcoming Budget. In the spring,
she decided to meet her borrowing rule by just £10 billion. The subsequent dilution of planned
reductions to the generosity of disability benefits, and the partial reversal of recent cuts to
pensioners’ winter fuel payments, will reduce this slender margin. A downgrade to the Office for
Budget Responsibility’s economic forecasts could easily eliminate it. If that happens, the
Chancellor will face three options: borrow more in breach of her fiscal rules, reduce spending or
increase taxation. The first of those options seems unlikely: the Chancellor has (understandably
and repeatedly) stated that she will not loosen the fiscal rules. As for spending reductions,
unpicking detailed departmental spending plans up to 2028–29 set out in June’s Spending
Review seems unlikely. Pencilling in cuts to total spending in 2029–30 (the year in which her
fiscal rules bind) in order to meet the letter of her targets would be possible but would stretch
credulity to breaking point. Cutting benefits has proven to be difficult politically. There is,
therefore, a widespread expectation that tax rises will be the key feature of the Budget. For that
reason, this chapter sets out the options for tax increases, but such increases are not inevitable.
Any changes to tax policy should be done in a way that, ideally, improves the design of the tax
system and that, at a minimum, does not worsen it.
Key findings
1. Tax revenue as a share of national income is set to reach a UK record high of
37.4% in 2026–27. This is still lower than in many other Western European countries.
It would be feasible for the Chancellor to raise more tax revenue if desired.
2. Raising the rates of income tax, National Insurance contributions (NICs) or VAT
– the three largest taxes – could straightforwardly raise large sums, but break
Labour’s manifesto promise not to ‘increase National Insurance, the basic, higher, or
additional rates of Income Tax, or VAT’. Extending the ongoing freeze in personal tax
thresholds would also raise a significant amount but (if it included a freeze to NICs
thresholds) would also break the manifesto pledge and would leave real-terms tax
thresholds – and the size of the tax rise – to be determined by the vagaries of future
inflation.
© The Institute for Fiscal Studies, October 2025

Options for tax increases 123
3. A new tax on income, hypothecated to a particular spending stream, may be a
more politically attractive way to increase tax on a large tax base, but would add
unnecessary complexity to the tax system. Hypothecation would either be
unjustifiably restrictive (such that spending on a particular item was tied to a specific
revenue stream) or economically meaningless (in the sense that the amount raised
from the tax bore no direct relation to the amount spent on an area).
4. Restricting income tax relief on pension contributions would raise large sums
but should be avoided. It would be unfair and distortionary to restrict up-front relief
but continue to tax pension income at the taxpayer’s marginal rate. It would also be
practically very difficult to attribute employer contributions to defined benefit
arrangements to specific individuals so that they could be taxed. There are better
options for increasing tax on pensions, including levying some NICs on employer
pension contributions and/or reforming the 25% tax-free element of pension income.
5. Labour’s pledge not to raise the biggest three taxes, if adhered to, seriously constrains
options. Raising significantly more revenue from the next four largest taxes –
corporation tax, council tax, business rates and fuel duties – would also bring
challenges.
6. The government has ruled out increasing the main or small profits rates of
corporation tax or reducing the main corporation tax reliefs. It has previously
stated its intention to shift some business rates from small retail properties to large
properties, in the context of the Labour party manifesto’s intention to ‘raise the same
revenue but in a fairer way’. Making tweaks to the current system rather than moving to
a land value tax for commercial property is a missed opportunity.
7. Council tax rates are already assumed to rise by 4.3% per year for the rest of the
parliament; larger increases would be needed to bring in additional revenue. An
alternative to raising all rates further would be to increase rates on homes in higher
value bands. This would make council tax less regressive. But because council tax
bands in England and Scotland are still based on 1991 valuations, the increases would
not accurately target the properties that are most valuable today. A sensible goal would
be to move to a tax that is proportional to up-to-date property valuations.
8. Current forecasts assume that rates of fuel duties (which are lower in cash terms than
they were in 2009) will in future keep pace with RPI inflation and that the current
‘temporary’ 5p per litre cut will come to an end – a combined cash increase of more
than 20% by 2029–30 on current forecasts. Anything less than that would lose the
government revenue relative to current forecasts.
© The Institute for Fiscal Studies, October 2025

Options for tax increases 124
9. Higher tax rates on income from capital – including rental income, dividends, interest
and self-employment profits – could raise money. So too could removing capital gains
forgiveness at death. But simply raising rates would discourage saving and
investment. The case for genuine reform is clear: improving the design of the tax base
(entailing some giveaways) and then more closely aligning overall tax rates across
different forms of income and gains would produce a fairer and more growth-friendly
system.
10. We caution against introducing an annual wealth tax, which would face huge
practical challenges. It would also penalise saving and, the more it was concentrated
on the very wealthy, the more it would incentivise them to leave (or not come to) the
UK. It would not be a well-targeted way to tax the large returns that wealth can
generate and, as such, would be no substitute for well-functioning taxes on capital
income and gains. If the Chancellor wants to raise more from the better-off, a
better approach would be to fix existing wealth-related taxes.
11. The Chancellor should not increase stamp duties (because they lead to asset
misallocation and lower labour mobility, dragging on growth in the process) or
insurance premium tax (which creates damaging distortions to production
decisions that weigh on productivity).
12. The overall tax gap (between taxes owed and revenues collected) has fallen, but the
tax gap for small companies has ballooned; reducing it should be a priority. The
approach should include compliance activities and improving policy design.
13. It would be difficult, but not impossible, for the Chancellor to raise tens of
billions of pounds more revenue without breaking Labour’s manifesto promise
not to increase National Insurance, the basic, higher or additional rates of income tax,
or VAT. Just because large sums could be raised elsewhere does not mean it would
be sensible. Many of the tax-raising options outside the ‘big three’ would have
particularly damaging effects on growth and welfare.
14. However much revenue she seeks to raise overall, the Chancellor could create a
fairer, simpler, more growth-friendly tax system. That means grasping the nettle
and pursuing genuine tax reform (property and capital taxes would both be good
places to start). At a minimum, the Chancellor should avoid measures that worsen the
design of the tax system.
© The Institute for Fiscal Studies, October 2025

Options for tax increases 125
4.1 Introduction
UK tax revenues have risen substantially in recent years and are forecast to reach an all-time high
of 37.4% of national income next year. Even so, if the Chancellor wants to raise more revenue
from taxes, that would be possible – as evidenced by the many Western European countries that
raise substantially more tax relative to the size of their economies than the UK does.
The tools available to a tax-raising Chancellor are many and varied. This chapter lays out the
main levers for raising revenue – which are summarised in Table 4.1 at the end – and discusses
the key features of different options. We consider the taxes that the Chancellor can control and,
for those taxes that are devolved, account for the effects of adjustments to devolved government
finances. A theme throughout is that the poor design of the tax system makes tax rises more
damaging than they need to be. However much revenue the Chancellor wishes to raise, we urge
her to take steps towards a fairer and more growth-friendly tax system.
The most straightforward way to raise large sums of revenue would be to increase the main rates
of income tax, National Insurance contributions (NICs) or value added tax (VAT). This should
not be surprising. These are by far the three largest taxes – together accounting for two-thirds of
total tax revenue (see Figure 4.1) – because they are levied on large tax bases. It is well known,
however, that Ms Reeves’s ability to raise these taxes will be seriously hampered by Labour’s
(self-imposed) political constraints. The party’s 2024 manifesto states that ‘Labour will not
increase taxes on working people, which is why we will not increase National Insurance, the
basic, higher, or additional rates of Income Tax, or VAT’.
What is perhaps less well known is that Ms Reeves also faces significant constraints if she wants
to raise money from any of the next four largest taxes – corporation tax, council tax, business
rates and fuel duties. She has made pledges that rule out raising the main corporation tax rate and
has signalled an intent to make revenue-neutral reforms to business rates. Council tax and fuel
duties are already assumed to rise significantly across the parliament, meaning that even greater
increases would be needed to improve the public finances.
This is not to say that the Chancellor cannot raise significant sums without raising the ‘big three’
taxes. As an illustration (but not a recommendation), she could raise £20 billion if she:
introduced a new council tax surcharge that acted to double council tax rates on band G and H
properties (£4.4 billion); abolished the residence nil-rate band within inheritance tax (£6 billion);
increased the bank surcharge to 6% (£1.2 billion); ended forgiveness of capital gains tax at death
(£2.3 billion); abolished business asset disposal relief (£0.9 billion); and reduced the small
companies tax gap by £5 billion. But if the Chancellor wants to raise large sums – and wants to
do so without having to raise many smaller taxes – she will likely need to turn to a tax on a large
base and therefore break, at least in spirit, her previous pledges.
© The Institute for Fiscal Studies, October 2025

bodies. These refunds (which are still classed by the Office for National Statistics as taxation) are included
in the ‘other taxes’ category. Figure for property taxes includes business rates, council tax and stamp duty
land tax (and its devolved equivalents). Corporation tax includes receipts from the bank surcharge, energy
profits levy and petroleum revenue tax. ‘Other indirect taxes’ includes fuel duties, tobacco duties, alcohol
duties, air passenger duty, insurance premium tax, the climate change levy, vehicle excise duties,
environmental levies (including the emissions trading scheme), customs duties, betting and gaming taxes,
the landfill tax, the aggregates levy, the soft drinks industry levy, the plastic packaging tax, vaping tax, the
residential property developer tax and the carbon border adjustment mechanism. Figure for capital taxes
